## Turn 1

**Owner, 01:39:30 UTC on November 21,** verbatim from this chat's log. The fenced list near its top is their paste of the closing lines of the November 19 chat's last message, which the harness wraps in a `pasted_content` tag; each line beginning with `>` after it is the owner quoting one of those questions, and what follows is their answer.

``````text
The 11/19 chat concluded with the following set of questions:

````
  

<pasted_content id="5da4">
- The five facts for the road to December 7–8
    (AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/README.md):
    - Is your work code under git or TFS version control?
    - Can the work machine reach the npm registry?
    - What may leave that machine, and where will you present from?
    - Roughly how much code is in the largest repository?
    - Which browser, and does it have a graphics card?
  - Git: a branch per dev day, merged into main only at verified landings — yes
    or no? The guide's Git rule is unchanged until you answer.
  - Work items: does the form serve?
</pasted_content id="5da4">

````

I have answers to all of them, but still feel like my lack of replies yesterday has left the trajectory steering a little bit askew. Let me open some stuff up and try to start responding.

>Is your work code under git or TFS version control?

Both. I have 2 git repos I work on and then several other code projects I either work on or my software interacts with which are part of TFS. The two git repos correspond to two public-facing websites: one for legal clients and one for consumers to make payments. The TFS repos correspond to basically anything that talks to anything on-prem. 

>Can the work machine reach the npm registry?

Sure but if I don't have to, that's much better. Like downloading an HTML file off of the Live Documentation hosted github pages site would be way cooler. Each copy of Live Documentation Explorer should be capable of writing a new fresh blank copy of Live Documentation Explorer, eagerly ready to take in its first scan!

>What may leave that machine, and where will you present from?

Nothing leaves the machine and I would just share my screen, which would mostly just be viewing my remote dev workstation. It's NOT heavily formal. This is just a "nice to have" for when I explain my portions of the cumulative applications estate of the enterprise because -- well -- that's the point of the software we're building!

>Roughly how much code is in the largest repository?

Any given scan-sized object is reasonable in size. I would scope to the solution level for most world map objects, where each world map object is a recursively scanned directory. No scanned object would even remotely approach a million lines. The t3 code scan is likely far more complex than any single scan I could do at my work. The engineers have separated out the applications really nicely in my opinion. Indeed, that's another thing about this software: it's clear to me that the more seasoned employees can see a cumulative architecture in their minds and it is sensible. But nobody has ever had the time to really draw it. And it changes frequently enough that we don't want to put in the effort to do so sometimes. 

>Which browser, and does it have a graphics card?

Absolute crap VM. No idea on graphics card, but we're talking about a VM that has 8GB of RAM and a handful of GB of remaining storage available. We want this to be able to run on absolute garbage, even if some amount of precomputation time with reasonable progress indicators is required to do so. 

>Git: a branch per dev day, merged into main only at verified landings — yes
    or no? The guide's Git rule is unchanged until you answer.

I'm fine with a branch per dev day. At my day job, we typically make a git branch per work item that we'd plan to get a code review on (PR or TFS code review request). But those repos are getting messy too. I am no git ninja, and I like to do the most vanilla thing and merge into Dev without squashing or rebasing, and when prod time comes, we merge into main without squashing or rebasing there either. So truthfully, I'm somewhat early in my journey towards git hygeine (despite using git for over 10 years), but I will say that the old-tech of TFS has rather impressed me with the simplicity of its mental model. 

>Work items: does the form serve?

It'll do for now. Not madly in love with it but I think it's a reasonable starting point. Whether or not it should be as simple as markdown files is not yet clear to me. 

---

But let me zoom out here and say that I suspect I've accumulated quite a few decisions/questions that were raised for me and have failed to answer them or allowed defaults (your educated choices) to take effect. It would be very helpful to me if you could dispatch some builders to mine the November 15th, 16th, 17th, 18th, and 19th chat logs to attempt to bubble up to _you_ everything I failed to answer (you can use quite small models for that) and then you mine the bubbled-up list for what has actually been superseded versus what is still relevant. (If the bubbled-up might-be-relevant list is too large, you can request a builder to work through that might-be-relevant list and narrow it down to the actually-relevant list, but I wouldn't choose something smaller than Opus 5.5 High for that bit). Finally, on the list of what is still relevant and worth investigating, I want you to investigate yourself first. Whether or not you choose to offer a preferred approach or choice is up to you, but I want to see you engage with these questions in a fresh context and I want you to see how much churn occurs in these questions, with new findings superseding old ones often. A sampling of this churn firsthand will, I think, help ground your expectations and hopefully shake out just a tad of the (in my opinion) overcertainty which made it into the work item markdown files (which again, are a good start, but we should still be open to something better if it appears for a similar level of simplicity). 

I see that the last chat led to a fair bit of certainty, but I've seen "blind playtests" with builders come back with feedback that can change our trajectory in ways that supersedes what we've discussed. Our software crystallizes further, but when you use it, you can still feel rough edges innumerable. Much remains to be done and much uncertainty still remains on the horizon. That uncertainty is not a bad thing -- it's just the possibility space remaining open to us. 

---

Oh, should we mark the dev day as 11/20 or 11/21? It's like 8:40 PM eastern for me. I'm happy to go either way.
``````

``````markdown
**Dev day:** I've named this one November 21. The record keeps UTC, where this chat opened at 01:39 on the 21st, and the 20th belongs wholly to the last dev day, which ran through it. Say so and I'll rename it. It is also the first day on its own branch, `day/2026-11-21`, as you agreed.

**What I read:** the guide, both READMEs, all four Memory documents, the five latest summaries and the vision, each to its last line; the eight work items; the November 19 measurement of a page that scans; and the open, close and pin proposal. I did not read the five records myself; the readers and judges below did.

## The mining

Nineteen Sonnet readers went through the five records and listed 652 askings. Three Opus builders at high effort merged the repeats and judged each against every message you typed in those days.

| Of 281 distinct things put to you, November 15 to 19 | |
| --- | ---: |
| You answered, in your own words | 85 |
| Overtaken before an answer was needed | 25 |
| Never really a question (status lines, notes to self) | 67 |
| No answer came and my predecessors' choice stands | 50 |
| You deferred, to playtests or to pictures | 20 |
| Open | 34 |

About 85 still wait once the overlap between days is removed. The count, the grouping and the three judged lists are in [the record of it](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/unanswered-questions/README.md).

The churn you wanted me to see:

- **Marbles:** asked for on the 16th, "a worthy new default" on the 18th, off by your own reversal on the 19th, with the pattern trial built and removed in one night.
- **Where the tool runs:** proposed as a self-copying page on October 3, package first on October 10, the page measured and recommended on November 19, "not on this road" on November 20, your stated preference today.
- **Our own lists:** nine statements in direction.md contradicted the record or the code, for example marbles still called the default in three places. I corrected each with its date.

Three faults on our side came out of the count:

- Each of the six small questions of November 15 was put to you seven to nine times before you said "not one by one".
- A quarter of what reached you as a question was not one.
- Most of the engine questions from the night of the 20th change nothing a person sees and were mine to decide. I have taken those back.

## What your answers changed

- **TFS.** Most of what you would map has no git, so the scan cannot ask what is ignored and there is no history to read. I read the code: without git, a .NET solution's `bin`, `obj` and `packages` folders would be mapped, every DLL as an asset, and no `.tfignore` is read.
- **"Nothing leaves the machine."** Two work items rested on you pasting a report back from work. That plan is gone; the tool has to say on screen what it could not read.
- **Size and the VM.** The board's "about ten thousand files" was a guess. The constraint is the machine, so I measured it.

I opened the built Explorer as that VM might, with no graphics card, the processor slowed, on two cores ([the probe and its pictures](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/weak-machine/README.md)):

| Map | 2D | 3D |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Estate sample (36 files) | whole within 5 s, then smooth | 23 frames a second |
| This repository (1,134 files) | drawn, 2 to 5 frames a second for at least the first 12 s | drawn, 2 frames a second |
| Any map, where the browser refuses WebGL | works | [a dead page with a stack trace](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/weak-machine/no-webgl--estate-3d--12s.png) |

This is my machine imitating a weak one, not yours. The last row is a defect whatever you decide. The mend is what designers call graceful degradation: a 3D link shows the same subject in 2D, and the 3D button says why it is not offered.

## What still waits, under the decisions beneath it

1. **Where the tool runs and where a scan's facts live.** This is the fork below.
2. **What a scan takes in** (submodules, `exclude` patterns, documents as nodes, build folders). You gave the principle already, that it is configurable, so the rest is mine to settle by measuring on real repositories.
3. **Open, close and pin, and what can be taken back.** One [proposal](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/open-close-pin/README.md) has waited unread since the 20th.
   - It is sound in that a click never lets go.
   - It keeps one thing it set out to cure: a click on a neighbour would still only select in 2D but grow the picture in 3D.
   - Nobody can judge it from a page of words. It needs a trial and strangers.
4. **What the page promises on a weak machine** (Settle Hold, the press, the 1,024-file limit, when 2D stops rearranging). All were weighed on a graphics card. Your "precomputation with progress indicators" is a direction none of the trials took.
5. **Small defaults of look.** This is the largest group and the least consequential. You deferred it to playtests; [the packet](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Screenshots/owner-packet/README.md) of twelve waits for whenever you choose.
6. **How the history is computed.** Mine, by measurement and independent review.
7. **How we work.** Mine, except a deny rule for `git push`, which is a setting only you can add. None exists, and I never push.

**Promised to you and never delivered:**

- You decided on November 16 that a shared link must not change the receiver's saved settings. It still does.
- The three-form trial of a closed directory's farthest look was never built.
- Alternatives for telling a whole-file pin from a flow pin were never brought.
- The pass on visual customization, which you made a condition, was never begun.

## Decisions for you

**1. Does the page come before the package?** This would reverse your order of October 10.

- **Page first (my recommendation).**
  - The next large build after the readers is the Explorer page that scans a folder you pick, shows its progress, keeps the facts inside itself and saves a blank or filled copy.
  - Nothing is installed, nothing is written into the folder, and a TFS folder works like any other.
  - [Measured on the 19th](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/execution-surface/README.md): the unchanged generator ran in a page with output identical to Node's, 5.6 MB.
  - It costs a file-access seam through about fifty places in the engine.
  - The page has no history, so your two git repositories would get theirs from the command line.
  - Not known: how long a scan takes in a tab on that VM, or what its browser allows.
- **Package first.** Fewer unknowns in the engine, but Node and 64 to 100 MB go onto a disk with a few GB, and it writes files somewhere.
- **A page that only views and saves itself, scanning by command line.** The least work, but Node is still needed there.

**2. Which browser is on that VM?** If it is Firefox, the page can read a folder but not write beside the code.

**3. Which shapes does your work code have most of?** Scripts calling each other's functions, scripts running SQL, user controls, or procedures named from C#. This was asked twice before and ranks what the readers mend next.

## What I changed

- **The guide's Git rule:** each dev day works on `day/<date>`; `main` moves only by an ordinary merge, never squashed or rebased. I dropped the earlier proposal's leave for builders to rebase, on your "most vanilla thing".
- **[The work items](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/README.md):** every date of ours is withdrawn and each issue now says what it rests on. No tooling is built while the form is on trial.
- **Memory:** your words of today are in owner.md and direction.md.
- **Housekeeping:** 13 leftover worktrees were removed after checking each, which took 36 GB down to 18.

Everything is committed on the day branch; `main` is unmoved.

## Running now

Three builders are out, none touching `main`:

- **The WebGL mend.** It is a defect whatever you decide.
- **The join of the SQL, PowerShell and .NET readers onto today's engine, on a branch.** They are needed whichever way the tool arrives, and they stood graded and unjoined for two days.
- **Blind playtests on today's build.** These include the narrative test you called "a true test" and a new task that traces a payment through the estate sample.

I started nothing that depends on your answer to the first decision.
``````

``````text
>1. Does the page come before the package?
>Page first (my recommendation)
>Package first.
>A page that only views and saves itself, scanning by command line.

Maybe this is naive of me, but this question surprises me a little:
1.  I have aspired to have a page that can write a page for a fair while and spoken it a fair few times. 
2. With really awesome architecture, this shouldn't feel like a death sentence to switch between one and another (CLI versus browser window). Explore the possibility space that enables both if possible and elegant with great architecture. If not possible, explore the possibility space of what can be done with a page. If not enough is possible, then fall back to CLI. 

>2. Which browser is on that VM? If it is Firefox, the page can read a folder but not write beside the code.

Chromium-based. Edge or Chrome. 

>3. Which shapes does your work code have most of? Scripts calling each other's functions, scripts running SQL, user controls, or procedures named from C#. This was asked twice before and ranks what the readers mend next.

And I keep not answering because it strikes me as beside-the-point. You have a good idea of the kind of possibility space you need to accommodate by permuting combinations of the ingredients I've described. I feel that if I allow this to be too specified, I will allow for early victory celebration well before we've achieved what we've set out to do. It is not in my best interest to make only my use case look pristine and then look in disbelief at others who somehow can't get the same level of quality out of the software. Trying to tackle a wide variety of codebase shapes and languages and connectivities was a big big part of the Copilot era of this workspace, and it was _incredibly_ worthwhile. So many shapes I did not imagine (as they came in languages I'd never used) appeared and presented with interesting important design challenges. Their constraints helped shape considerably what we do and don't support, how deep or extensive our data model is, etc. Put another way, exploring a wide variety of possibility space enabled (forced, really) more parsimonious and simple design to accommodate the ability to measure and detect honestly in more contexts.
``````

### Root's reply to Turns 2 and 3, 03:17:32 UTC on November 21, as delivered

Verbatim from this chat's log.

``````markdown
I've taken your two messages as direction: both surfaces from one engine, the page alone if that fails, the command line alone last. Putting it to you as a fork was my mistake; the mining I'd just done showed you asking for the page on October 3, November 19 and today. Nothing below is waiting on you.

## The architecture I'm exploring

There are three ways to get both, and I lean to the second:

1. **Swap Node's parts out when the page is built.** This is what the November 19 probe did, and the engine needs no change. But the engine does not know it has two homes, and I found today that it drifts (see the measurements).
2. **One small interface, two hosts.** The engine asks its host for three things: list the folder, give me this file, write this. Designers call this "ports and adapters". About fifty places in the engine change.
3. **A scan as a pure function over a snapshot.** The host loads everything into memory and the engine touches no disk at all. It is the simplest to reason about, but it would read your packages folders whole.

```
              a folder of code
                     │
        ┌────────────┴────────────┐
    Node host                browser host
   (disk, git)           (the folder you pick)
        └────────────┬────────────┘
   list → choose what to leave out → read
                     │
                the engine
                     │
        ┌────────────┴────────────┐
  Markdown on disk         facts kept in the page
        └────────────┬────────────┘
             one Explorer page
     (shows the map; saves a blank or filled copy)
```

- **One page, two ways to fill it.** The command line would write the same HTML file the page saves, already filled. Either copy can scan again or save a blank one.
- **The listing comes before any reading.** Your ignore patterns need that step anyway.
- **What a page cannot do** is anything that runs git: a repository's history and changed-only runs. Your two git repositories would get history from the command line, written into the same page. TFS folders have none on either surface.
- **What would overturn it:** a reader that can't take its files through the interface without a second copy of its logic, or a scan too slow on your VM to bear even with a progress indicator.

## What I measured today

I rebuilt the November 19 scanning page from today's engine and timed it ([the write-up](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/scan-in-a-tab/README.md)). "Slowed" is my machine imitating a weak one, not your VM.

| Folder | Files | Scan in a tab | Slowed 4×, two cores | Tab's peak memory |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Estate sample | 90 | 0.3 s | 1.6 s | 234 MB |
| DNN Modules (C#, web forms) | 845 | 3.9 s | 15.9 s | 299 MB |
| DNN Library (C#) | 1,413 | 20.9 s | 80.2 s | 586 MB |

- **The page probe broke within two days.** It failed three times before it ran, each on something new the engine asked of Node, and nothing had flagged it.
- **The page draws nothing while it scans.** The tab would look hung for the whole scan, so the scan must hand control back between files for a progress indicator to move.
- **Each file is read four to six times.** One reading would cut the work most on the weakest machine.
- **Time looks bearable at solution size:** seconds to a minute or two. Under heavy load the slowed 845-file scan took 59 s instead of 16.

## Your other points

- **Shapes.** I won't ask again. What the readers mend next is ranked by breadth across real codebases, each measured against its language's own parser.
- **Ignore patterns before a scan.** This is a new task: list the folder without reading it, show what would be scanned by folder with counts and which rule leaves each thing out, let you change it, then scan. The patterns would be the same on both surfaces, with `.tfignore` read where one exists. It will come to you as pictures and a trial.
- **100k files.** Today's probe page reads every file before scanning, which would choke on your packages folders. That is why the choice has to come before any reading.

The work items now say this, mainly [how the tool arrives](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/tool-arrives-on-windows.md) and [what a scan leaves out](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/scan-leaves-no-trace.md). Your words are in Memory, and everything is committed on the day branch.

## Running now

Four builders are out, none touching `main`:

- **The page check (new).** A standing test that the engine, bundled for a browser, maps every kind of code we read exactly as Node does. It also keeps a list of what the engine still needs from Node, so drift shows up in review.
- **The readers' join.** SQL and PowerShell are joined onto today's engine with every check green and the SQL scores matching their record; .NET is being merged. The scripts that scored PowerShell on November 19 were lost with the old container, so the builder is rebuilding them as a committed command.
- **Blind playtests (interim).**
  - Asked to trace a payment through the estate sample, all six testers got the chain of systems right. None named a file, because none went below the World Map.
  - The history scrubber's first button was taken for "jump to start" and moved one step.
  - The hub's staging address read as a misroute to the two strongest testers.
  - Arriving on a pinned file still cuts its neighbours off.
  - The full write-up with pictures comes when the builder finishes.
- **The WebGL mend.** Still running.
``````

And scip-dotnet's symbols do not carry a generic type's arity, so it cannot grade the alias finding: the compiler's own binding, through the November 19 probe's program, is committed as the C# grader with a command of its own, which the work item's "each language's grader runs by one command" already asked for.

That last is root's own find of this morning: [`oracle.test.ts`](../../../../../tests/integration/live-docs/oracle.test.ts) says of itself that it "asserts the bookkeeping, not the adapter's score", so no reader's regression on a sample program can fail the gate, and three of the estate's twenty hand-verified edges are missing with nothing saying so.

What it measured on DNN (10,776 files; 39.0 MB as compact JSON): strings repeat heavily (475,296, of which 109,035 distinct); the median file has no wire in or out, the hub has 632 dependents; three of the four real questions read a few percent of the whole. Five designs, each read back identical field by field and written twice to the same bytes: JSON gzipped (2.74 MB, first answer 1.9 s); a binary layout of our own read with nothing but a browser's built-ins (2.21 MB, 117 ms); SQLite through Node's built-in module (29.6 MB, 95 ms from memory and 20 ms read in place); sorted lines of text, one fact a line, gzipped (2.80 MB, 820 ms, and `grep` and `diff` work on it); the lines cut into parts named by their hash (4.87 MB). The owner's HDF5 fails two values outright, by measurement: it stamps times into the file (32 bytes differ between two writes) and cannot compress strings of varying length. SQLite's three reasons for leaving in January 2026, each tested against today: the duplication expires if it is the one store, its consumer's end does not apply, the native module's pain expired with Node's built-in. Root added to the next leg what the owner said today that the brief could not hold: a size five times DNN's, slowed in a tab; whether each design can be written as facts arrive one file at a time, which the engine's proposal assumes; what a stopped scan leaves; and the question of what "the same bytes for the same facts" is for now that the stored form is not committed. Its lean is provisional and root has not adopted it.

At 04:00:40 UTC:

``````markdown
The first hard evidence on your architecture question is in: the engine already maps code identically in a browser page and under Node, for almost everything we have.

## The page check

A standing test now bundles the engine for a browser and maps every sample folder twice, once in a real Chromium page and once under Node, comparing every output file. I re-ran it myself before merging it into the day branch.

| | |
| --- | ---: |
| Sample folders mapped both ways | 45 |
| Readers exercised | all 19 |
| Identical output | 43 |
| Differing | 2 (six files, one cause) |
| Scan time over all 45, page / Node | 6.3 s / 9.6 s |

- **The one cause:** the engine asks TypeScript's Node host whether filenames are case-sensitive. A page has no such host, and Windows and Linux answer differently, so it was also a hidden difference between machines. The builder is replacing it with a fixed rule: names are read as written.
- **Drift now shows up.** The bundle refuses to build if the engine imports a Node module that has no browser replacement, and a committed list of what the scan needs from Node changes visibly in review.
- **It will catch the readers' join.** The PowerShell reader uses Node's crypto module for a cache key; I've sent that to the readers' builder to replace before the join lands.

## What the engine needs from Node today

This is counted from every call made during those 45 scans:

- **Path arithmetic:** the bulk of calls, and it needs no host at all.
- **"Is this a file?" probes:** 2,163 of them, asked synchronously. A browser's folder handle can only answer asynchronously, so these must be answered from a listing taken up front.
- **File reads:** 2,144. Each file is read 3 to 5 times, and one `go.mod` 30 times.
- **Folder walks:** five per scan.
- **Writes:** only the generator makes them.

So the interface is small: list the folder once, answer "is this a file" from that listing, read a file's bytes, and write results somewhere. The listing-first step your ignore patterns need turns out to be the step a page needs anyway.

The same builder is now writing that interface up as a design document for you to read, with the alternatives and what would overturn it. It builds nothing beyond the case-sensitivity fix.

The numbers and the per-folder table are in [the builder's account](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/page-check/README.md).

Still running: the readers' mends, the WebGL mend, the format research, and this design write-up.
``````

At 04:23:09 UTC:

``````markdown
The page now writes exactly what Node writes for all 45 sample folders, and the "one engine, two hosts" design is written up for you to read: [engine-and-hosts.mdmd.md](.mdmd/layer-3/engine-and-hosts.mdmd.md). It is a proposal; nothing of it is built except the check that measures it.

## What changed

- **The ledger of differences is empty.** The one engine cause is mended: filename case is now the engine's own fixed rule (names are read as written) instead of a question put to the host machine. I re-ran all 45 browser cases on the merged branch and they pass.
- **The design document is merged.** The page check's builder drafted it from every call it counted, and I read and corrected it.

## What the design says

- **What a host gives the engine:** a listing of the folder's file names, a file's bytes on request, and optionally just a file's first bytes.
- **What a host takes back:** the scan's facts as data, and word of how far the scan has come.
- **Listing first:** the folder is listed before anything is read. What gets left out is decided on that listing by one function both hosts share, drawing on `.gitignore`, `.tfignore`, a starting list of build folders and your own patterns. That choice can be shown to you and changed before the scan runs.
- **Each file read once.** Today it is three to five times.
- **What cannot cross into a page:** anything that runs git. The history is computed by the command line and reaches the page as facts.
- **The road:** nine steps, each removing one of the browser stand-ins, with the page check proving after each that nothing changed.
- **Alternatives and unknowns:** section 7 sets it beside three alternatives. Section 8 lists what would overturn it, chiefly scan time and memory at your scale on your VM, and whether your company's browser policy allows the folder picker.

## My two corrections

- **The listing carries names only.** The draft had it carry each file's size and modified time, but a page can only learn those by opening each file, which on 100k files is the cost the listing exists to avoid.
- **The scan reports progress and hands control back between files.** You asked for progress indicators, and today a scan in a tab freezes the page until it ends.

Building the interface waits for the readers' join to land, since it touches every reader's file.

Still running: the readers' mends, the WebGL follow-ups, and the format research.
``````

At 04:39:58 UTC:

``````markdown
Six builders are running and nothing is waiting on you. There is one thing for your eye when you have a minute, at the end.

## The format research: first numbers

The research on how a scan's facts should be stored has finished its first leg. It measured five designs on a real scan of DNN (10,776 files, 39 MB as plain JSON). Each design read back identical to the facts it was given, and wrote the same bytes twice.

| Design | Size on disk | Time to first answer | What it buys |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| JSON, gzipped (the baseline) | 2.74 MB | 1.9 s | Nothing; every question reads everything |
| A compact binary layout of our own | 2.21 MB | 0.12 s | Smallest; needs nothing a browser lacks |
| SQLite | 29.6 MB | 0.02–0.10 s | Fastest; you could query it without our tool |
| Sorted lines of text, one fact per line, gzipped | 2.80 MB | 0.82 s | `grep` and `diff` work on it; five commits of history fit in 1.92 MB against 5.16 MB as JSON |
| The same lines, cut into shared parts | 4.87 MB | 0.08 s | Less than hoped; sharing parts saved 25% where line differences saved 63% |

- **HDF5 fails two of your values by measurement.** It stamps the time into every file, so two writes of the same facts differ by 32 bytes, and it cannot compress text of varying length.
- **SQLite is a real candidate again.** It was dropped in January mainly because its native module broke on Windows. Node now ships SQLite built in, so that reason has expired.
- **Nothing is chosen.** These numbers are from Node only. The next leg measures each design inside a browser tab, slowed to resemble your VM, at five times DNN's size, and checks whether each can be written as a scan proceeds and survive a closed tab.

## The readers

- **I reversed one of my own rulings.** I had told the builder to reset the history's memory estimate whenever the code changes. It showed that every upgrade would then start from a default guess, which on an 8 GB machine risks running out of memory. The estimate now carries across code changes.
- **Two of our C# graders shared the reader's blind spots.** One reads project references by the same text pattern the reader uses, so it agreed with the reader's mistake on a commented-out reference. The other cannot tell `Helper` from `Helper<T>`. Both will now take their answers from Microsoft's build system and the C# compiler directly.
- **The nine mends are split across three builders** (C# and ASP.NET, PowerShell, SQL), since they touch different files.
- **A gap I found in our own tests is being closed.** The test that compares readers against their graders on sample code only checks that the counts add up, so a reader getting worse could never fail it. Three connections we verified by hand on the estate sample are missing today, and nothing reports it.

## Started: choosing what a scan leaves out

This is your ask from earlier ("a good solid way to create ignore patterns on what they scan, before they scan it"). A builder is writing the single function, shared by the page and the command line, that decides what is left out from a folder's listing before any file is read.

- **For `.gitignore`, git is the judge.** The function must match git's own answer, rule for rule, on generated cases and on real repositories.
- **For `.tfignore`, Microsoft's documentation leaves three rules unstated.** The builder is checking whether Microsoft's open-source TFS client answers them in its code. Whatever cannot be established stays marked unknown.
- **It will also produce the listing you would see** on a folder built to your shape (over 100k files, mostly packages), with counts by folder and by rule. I will draw the choice screen from that.

## For your eye: how the Local Map arrives on a file

Strangers were tested on two ways of arriving, and they pull in opposite directions:

- **[Reading Size](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/blind-playtests/estate-uses-sonnet-medium-look-only/shots/001-look.png)** (today's default, your direction of November 4): the file's card is large and its description is readable, but the neighbours are cut off at both edges. Three of four strangers answered correctly.
- **[Neighbours](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/blind-playtests/estate-uses-sonnet-medium-arrival-neighbours/shots/001-look.png)**: all three folders and every connection are whole and the names are still readable, but the descriptions are gone. Eight of eight answered correctly, in fewer steps.

Your idea from the 19th, arriving at reading size and then pulling back, could not be tested this way, because the testers only see the picture after it settles. I have left Reading Size as the default. You can try all three on your preview under Tuning → 2D Layout → Arrival. Tell me which your eye prefers.
``````

At 05:26:34 UTC:

``````markdown
The format research's browser measurements are in, and they set up one choice that is yours: how much it is worth for the stored facts to be readable without our tool. Nothing is built on it until the readers land, so there is no hurry.

## What was measured

Seven formats held the same scan and each read back identical. The numbers below are from a Chromium tab slowed four times on two cores, as a stand-in for your VM (it ranks the formats; it does not predict your machine). "10k" is DNN's 10,776 files. "50k" is DNN repeated five times, which is not a real codebase and flatters formats that store each string once.

| Format | Opens at 10k: first answer, whole map | Opens at 50k: first answer, memory | Saved page at 10k | Readable without our tool |
| --- | --- | --- | ---: | --- |
| JSON, gzipped (today) | 3.2 s (both) | 25.8 s, 1.3 GB | 3.65 MB | Yes |
| Compact binary of our own | 0.15 s, 2.2 s | 1.0 s, 351 MB | 2.83 MB | No |
| Indexed text, one fact per line | 0.08 s, 15.7 s | 0.6 s, then 1.7 GB for the whole map | 3.98 MB | Yes: `zcat`, `grep`, `diff` |
| SQLite | 1.6 s, 5.0 s | 7.2 s, 819 MB | 6.19 MB | Yes: any SQLite tool |
| HDF5 | 0.8 s, 2.6 s, after 6.7 s to load its library | 1.5 s, 395 MB | 8.82 MB | Yes: HDF5 tools |

- **JSON is out.** At 50k files it needs 26 seconds and 1.3 GB before it can answer anything.
- **HDF5 is out.** It writes different bytes every time for the same facts, and its library adds 5.6 MB to every saved page.
- **SQLite is the best on the command line and the worst in a page.** Read from disk by Node it gives the fastest first answer of any format, in the least memory. Written inside a page at 50k it takes 854 MB and over two minutes. Since you want both surfaces from one design, I count it out.

## The two that remain

- **Compact binary.** A table of contents, the connections as plain arrays of numbers, each string stored once, each section compressed separately. It is the only format whose numbers stay in proportion at 50k, and it has the smallest saved page. The cost is that nobody can read it without our reader.
- **Indexed text.** One fact per line, sorted by path, in compressed chunks with a small index. For example, one line lists the 632 files that depend on `Globals.cs`, so `grep` answers "what depends on this". It answers a first question as fast as the binary. Loading the whole map is slow and memory-hungry, but that was measured with the simplest possible reader, so it is not yet a fair comparison.

## Your decision

1. **Binary as the stored form, text exported on demand (my lean).** Fastest and lightest on a weak machine. You run one command or press one button when you want something to `grep`.
2. **Indexed text as the stored form.** Always readable with ordinary tools, about 40% larger, slower to load a whole map. How much slower is what the next leg measures.

I lean to the first because your stated priority is running "on absolute garbage", and you already expected Markdown to be an export. Which matters more to you: the speed and memory, or never needing our tool to read the facts?

## Still being measured

- **A closed tab.** Today a scan running in a tab loses everything if the tab closes, in every format. The next leg builds a page that saves as it goes and resumes.
- **A fair reader for the indexed text,** so the two finalists are compared at their best.
- **The page saving a copy of itself** with the facts inside, at 50k files.

The full account is [the research README](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Research/stored-form/README.md); its first table is the two-minute version.
``````

``````markdown
## The two formats, each at its best

Measured side by side in a tab slowed four times on two cores; "50k" is DNN repeated five times, not a real codebase.

| | Compact binary | Indexed text |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 10k files: first answer, whole map, memory | 0.2 s, 2.0 s, 215 MB | 0.17 s, 7.0 s, 250 MB |
| 50k files: first answer, whole map, memory | 0.83 s, 11.0 s, 350 MB | 0.26 s, 27.6 s, 537 MB |
| Saved page at 50k | about 10 MB | about 20 MB |
| Readable without our tool | No | Yes: `zcat`, `grep`, `diff` |

- **Most of the text format's earlier cost was its reader, not the format.** With a careful reader its 1.7 GB became 537 MB and its 69 seconds became 27.6.
- **What remains is the format's own price:** about 2.5 times the time to load a whole map, 1.5 times the memory, and a saved page twice the size, in exchange for a file ordinary tools can read.
- **My lean is unchanged** (binary stored, text exported on demand), but it is now a real choice between two workable formats.

## A page that keeps its scan, and saves itself

This is your "page that writes a page", shown possible as an experiment; none of it is in the product yet.

- **A closed browser no longer loses the scan.** A page opened from disk saved each finished piece as it went. The browser was closed at 60%; a new browser found the saved work in about 4 seconds and finished the scan. The result was byte-for-byte what an uninterrupted scan writes. Saving as it goes cost no extra memory and at most 15% more time.
- **The page saved a copy of itself with the facts inside** in under half a second at 50k files. The copy was 10 MB, opened from disk, and answered questions.
- **Not measured:** saving into a folder you pick (its dialog cannot be driven by automation), how much storage your VM's browser will allow, and a browser killed outright rather than closed.

## Choosing what a scan leaves out

The deciding function is merged; nothing calls it yet.

- **`.gitignore`:** it agrees with git on every one of 48,931 paths across three real folders.
- **`.tfignore`:** Microsoft's open-source TFS client answered the three rules its documentation leaves out. A subfolder's file adds to its parent's, names match without case, and the last matching line wins.
- **A decision I made, for you to overrule:** a left-out folder is skipped whole, never looked inside. TFS itself has two behaviours here; I took the one that guarantees your packages folders are never even listed. If a `!` line might have rescued something inside, the listing will say so.
- **A warning already:** GitHub's standard Visual Studio ignore list would wrongly leave out 60 real DNN source files, because DNN has source folders named `Log` and `Packages`. So default leave-outs cannot go by folder name alone. Five candidate defaults are being measured on a folder built to your shape.

## The readers

- **PowerShell:** all five mends accepted. PowerShell itself corrected my ruling on aliases. Four real codebases score exactly as before, minus two false notices.
- **SQL:** accepted, as reported earlier; it is now building the test ledger for the compiler-checked samples.
- **C#:** still on its last three findings.

The details are on the work items: [stored format](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/tool-arrives-on-windows.md#a-scans-facts-in-one-stored-form-written-by-either-host), [leave-outs](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/scan-leaves-no-trace.md#choosing-what-a-scan-leaves-out-before-it-scans-on-either-surface), [readers](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/readers-on-main.md).
``````

``````markdown
## One thing you could settle in thirty seconds

When a script runs `& ./tools/build` and both `build` (no extension) and `build.ps1` exist, we draw the line to `build`, which is what PowerShell 7 on Linux does. The reviewer believes Windows runs `build.ps1`; I recall the opposite; neither of us has a Windows machine. A builder is reading PowerShell's source for the answer.

On your own Windows machine (not the work one), this shows it without running either file:

```powershell
mkdir t; cd t; 'x' > build; 'x' > build.ps1
``````

``````markdown
I searched our whole record for what was already decided about showing which way a connection runs, and it turned up three things that change the picture. One is a question for you.

## 1. The World Map's moving dashes run against the request

On the estate's board, the panel for a wire says "sqlserver calls oracle", but the dashes on that wire travel from oracle to sqlserver. For your own demo, a payment's dashes run from the databases back towards the portal.

- **September 28:** the dashes ran "the way the request goes", and you wrote "I absolutely adore the motion from outputs to inputs".
- **September 29:** when the colours were swapped to "blue offers, green uses", the agent also turned every wire round and reported it in one line; you were not asked.

I checked both in the record and in today's code.

**Question:** on the World Map, should the dashes run with the request (portal → API → router → service → databases) or with the offering, as now? I recommend with the request, since that board is where a person follows one. The colours would not change. I'll have it built as a switch so you can see both.

## 2. "Wires revealed on hover" was never your rejection

Our notes said this was "off the table by the record". The source shows it was an agent's analysis in March, about one old layout where revealing wires made the whole picture shift. Your own words in November 2025 praised hover-only lines.

It says nothing against a wire that is already drawn answering when you rest on it or press it. I corrected the note in [direction.md](AI-Agent-Workspace/Memory/direction.md).

## 3. The fix for "which way does this line run" is partly a rule we already have

On November 19 you ruled that words should wait for a pointer that rests. The World Map's wires already do this. The 2D map's wires do nothing: they cannot be pointed at, pressed or reached by keyboard.

## What I plan to build as one trial setting

1. **As now:** colour and side only.
2. **A wire answers:** rest on it, press it or tab to it, and it says one line where it is, such as "`PaymentService.cs` uses `PaymentsContext.cs`". This would also replace the six different wordings the screen uses today for the same relation.
3. **Chevrons along the wire,** pointing from what is offered to what uses it (our direction).
4. **Chevrons the other way,** as a dependency arrow points. This is against your left-to-right rule and is in the trial only to learn which habit strangers bring. Most developers read an arrow from A to B as "A uses B", the opposite of how our wires run, so a stronger arrow in our direction might make people more confidently wrong.
5. **Motion on what you point at:** dashes flowing along the wires of the card under a rested pointer. This is your own wish from September 28, never built. Testers only see stills, so this one is for your eye.

Arrowheads have never been shown to you on the 2D map; they were only ruled out for 3D (by you) and for the World Map (in an agent's brief).

The full findings, with every quote and its location, are in [the research note](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/line-direction/README.md). The alternatives are on [the work item](AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/stranger-follows-a-request.md).
``````

``````markdown
## The search defect

Six testers were asked what the folder `packages/engine/src/live-docs/history` holds (32 files). Every one typed its name or path into search first ([picture](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/blind-playtests/repo-open-history-sonnet-medium-cut-counted/shots/005-scroll.png)):

- There is no row for the folder itself when you type its path; typing just `history` does offer it.
- Three testers answered with the 12 and were wrong. Three searched letter by letter, 46 to 60 actions each.

The cause is in the search's scoring: a folder is matched only by its last name segment, and the file cap is silent. I'm treating this as a defect, not a design question, since the field itself says "Search files, symbols and directories".

One consequence: the edge counters are still untested by strangers. Nobody opened the folder from the map, so no counter ever appeared in those six runs.
``````

``````markdown
- **One control was misread by every tester:** the switch "Leave out build output and packages". They took it for the reason things are left out. One unticked it to keep a single folder and got the only wrong answer.
- **To leave a folder out in By reason, every tester typed its path as a pattern.** They all got it right, but these testers are models and type paths readily; a person may not.

## The design I drew from it

One screen, not two layouts:

1. **By reason is the screen.** This is *overview first, details on demand*: three or four rows say everything, and each opens only when asked.
2. **Leaving out by pointing moves into its right-hand column.** Each folder and each file kind under "Will be read" gets a quiet minus, the opposite of the plus on the left. So "don't read the 1,325 `.png` files" or "skip the Tests folder" is one press, with no pattern to type.
3. **The misread switch goes.** The Keep button on the "Build output" and "NuGet packages" rows already does its job.

The tree stays reachable by a link parameter so you can still compare. Your eye decides; I'll show pictures when the builder reports.
``````

``````text
I'm working my way from old messages to new ones trying my best to reply to what I see.

>"Test" for every language: a change made on PowerShell's evidence altered what counts as a test everywhere, so test-utils.tsx stopped being one. I've confined it to PowerShell files.

Where else do you see builders making regressions like this? Should any architecture be in place to scope test file matching patterns in some way? 

>Arrival, tried on strangers (20 runs)

Yep, these results undoubtedly indicate that arriving on the whole neighborhood allows fuller intuition in fewer actions on these "strangers" who reason over screenshots. (Hopefully you attempted to account for the incredibly severe bias that all LLMs have which favors _more_ text on the screen in nearly all scenarios). But what I had described was an automatic zoom-out from the single focused pinned item to its neighborhood, ensuring that the user knew the full detail available _and_ that the user started with less outer visual context and _grew into it_ automatically. Still few actions -- the actions are just taken automatically. However, we attempted to build this at least once and found that, due to jarring performance hits caused by breakpoint transitions, the start-framing-only-one-card-then-zoom-to-neighborhood approach failed on complex scenes (for very very simple scenes it was honestly not bad at all; the perf hit and crossing of breakpoints is what was jarring and broke that for us).

>Nobody read the drawing. Every tester handed the whole neighbourhood went straight to the reader's list of dependents.

As in all went to the detail panel? All went to the modal text representation of the card? This is ambiguous to me.

>The first hard evidence on your architecture question is in: the engine already maps code identically in a browser page and under Node, for almost everything we have.

Awesome!

>Drift now shows up. The bundle refuses to build if the engine imports a Node module that has no browser replacement, and a committed list of what the scan needs from Node changes visibly in review.

How do we detect this? In addition, if a browser offers something, do we _need_ a node module for that something? Put another way: wherever you see yourself reaching for two implementations to maintain for the same problem, ask whether the right architectural choices have been made. Maintaining two parallel implementations of the nitty-gritty guts sounds like an architecturally poor choice and a good way to cause drift no matter what.

>What cannot cross into a page: anything that runs git. The history is computed by the command line and reaches the page as facts.

Fair. I can understand why that might be unavailable for the pure browser version, but do ponder whether there is some way we can have our cake and eat it too. Git is just a hidden directory of files. Complex as can be, but we can read files from the browser...

>Alternatives and unknowns: section 7 sets it beside three alternatives. Section 8 lists what would overturn it, chiefly scan time and memory at your scale on your VM, and whether your company's browser policy allows the folder picker.

I work in web dev. My two git repos are actually WEBFORMS!!!! Can you believe it?! I know, old tech for sure! We don't use any of the stupid webforms postback crap or OnClick crap, we just use it as a simple way to have C# do a bit of preformatting on simple pages, and then we let on-page fetches handle the rest of being dynamic. They're super vanilla frontends -- no funny JS frameworks or fancy sass stylings -- just aspx, js, and css (with bootstrap for simple components). But anyway, I have free reign on what the browser can do _inside_ my dev machine. I necessarily need to have that ability for my specific work.

>The format research: first numbers
>SQLite is a real candidate again. It was dropped in January mainly because its native module broke on Windows. Node now ships SQLite built in, so that reason has expired.

Awesome! But does can it work in the browser? Otherwise the "compact binary layout of our own" seems like the better of the candidates in that table. 

>Two of our C# graders shared the reader's blind spots. One reads project references by the same text pattern the reader uses, so it agreed with the reader's mistake on a commented-out reference. The other cannot tell Helper from Helper<T>. Both will now take their answers from Microsoft's build system and the C# compiler directly.

Not while the browser-based software is running though, right? We don't have any ability to do anything like that right? (And I think we generally wouldn't want to -- a build can _do things_ depending on the framework or language you're working in -- the wasm tree-sitter was brought in to help avoid this)

>Started: choosing what a scan leaves out
>For .gitignore, git is the judge. The function must match git's own answer, rule for rule, on generated cases and on real repositories.
>For .tfignore, Microsoft's documentation leaves three rules unstated. The builder is checking whether Microsoft's open-source TFS client answers them in its code. Whatever cannot be established stays marked unknown.
>It will also produce the listing you would see on a folder built to your shape (over 100k files, mostly packages), with counts by folder and by rule. I will draw the choice screen from that.

I think that if `.gitignore` and `.tfignore` have very similar conventions (they do), then what we'd really be doing is -- after a user has pointed to a directory for scanning -- indicate that an ignore file was found, and then ask the user if they want to use (replace current ignore pattern in browser window), integrate (add to current ignore pattern in browser window), or ignore (make no change to current ignore pattern in browser window) the detected ignore file for that scan. The ignore pattern in the browser window must be easy to reuse, come with good available defaults (i.e. the standard Visual Studio gitignore) to optionally prefill with, and work functionally the same as a gitignore would. The list the user sees for ignore patterns is the source of truth for our software, and we can take that in from other ignore patterns as needed. 

>Your idea from the 19th, arriving at reading size and then pulling back, could not be tested this way, because the testers only see the picture after it settles. I have left Reading Size as the default.

Which unfortunately makes the test not an excellent test. You've learned important information, but only important information that can be gleaned from _one_ thorough inspection of an image. The human operating the software will exercise a different attention pattern: they will inspect the images with almost _no_ thoroughness at all, but they will _navigate_ and _move_ and _change_ and _integrate_ over many many pictures. I think your "action counts" metric is perhaps more useful as a proxy? Even then there is massive bias to account for in the LLM playtesting protocol.

>Commented-out XML is no longer read as real.

Phew!

>SQLite is the best on the command line and the worst in a page. Read from disk by Node it gives the fastest first answer of any format, in the least memory. Written inside a page at 50k it takes 854 MB and over two minutes. Since you want both surfaces from one design, I count it out.

Dang! Good to know. Wow the compact binary of our own is looking better and better.

>Binary as the stored form, text exported on demand (my lean). Fastest and lightest on a weak machine. You run one command or press one button when you want something to grep.

Agreed. Yeah looks like this will have to be our lean.

I personally lean towards the "copy of itself" for portability purposes
``````

At 18:21:39 UTC:

``````markdown
Answers to each point, in your order. Four pieces of work follow from them; I'm starting those now.

## Readers

- **How the commented-out reference lapsed.** The readers that parse a syntax tree have always skipped comments. The project-file reader never had a tree: since 2026-09-28 it has run one text pattern over the raw `.csproj`. Its grader used the same kind of pattern, so both agreed. Today's review found it by trying it, and its second pass found the same fault in ASP.NET markup. Both are fixed, but nothing yet guards every pattern-based reader; I'm adding that test.
- **"Test" for every language.** [archetype.ts](packages/engine/src/live-docs/archetype.ts#L104-L116) holds one list of every language's naming rules and applies it to every file. The C# rule `FooTests.cs` therefore also catches a TypeScript `runTests.ts`. Today's fix is an `if (powershell)` inside that list, which is a patch. The architecture I'm building: each language's reader declares what its own test runner treats as a test, applied to that language's files only and measured against the runner's own discovery. One table test across all languages makes a change for one that moves another fail.
- **Where else today.** The same shape, shared code changed on one case's evidence, happened four more times:
  - The readers' join removed the premise of four browser tests; only the full suite caught it.
  - A larger graph crossed the 3D budget and veiled the main group; the suite caught it.
  - The scanning-page probe broke three times in two days; nothing caught it until today's check.
  - The World Map dashes were turned round in September; nothing caught it for two months.
- **Graders and builds.** Correct. MSBuild and the compiler run only on our bench, by hand, to produce answer keys we keep as records. The product never builds, restores or runs the code it maps; it only reads text, on either surface.
- **Your Windows run settles it.** `./build` ran `build.ps1`, as PowerShell's source said and against my memory. The reader will follow Windows.

## Explorer

- **Arrival.** The bias is only partly accounted for. We count what testers do, not what they wish for, but a model reads a dense still completely at no cost, and a person does not. What you described is the "Reading, Then Neighbours" value, which is in Tuning now. The testers cannot see its motion, and I have not measured that motion on a complex scene. I'm having it measured on this repository, with the usual cure as an alternative for your eye: hold the cards' detail level while the camera moves and change it once at rest. Designers call the jolt you saw "level-of-detail popping".
- **"The reader's list".** That was my loose word for the Detail Panel, the side panel that opens when you click a file's name. Its Dependents section lists in words what uses the file. All twelve testers answered from there, so those runs say nothing about whether the drawing is understood.
- **The test's limits.** Agreed. From now these runs report actions and routes first and are used to find stumbles. They will not choose between two looks or judge motion; those go to your eye.
- **12 of 32.** No, it was a fixed cap of twelve rows with no sign; as far as I can read the code, search ignores the kind filters. It is fixed on `main`: the folder has its own row, and a cut list shows a count that opens the rest. I have not checked what happens when you choose a result the filters hide.

## One engine, two surfaces

- **How drift is detected.** A test bundles the engine for a browser and fails on any Node import that has no stand-in. A script also derives which Node functions each engine file uses and compares that with [a committed list](tests/page-scan/node-needs.json), so a new dependence shows as a changed line in review.
- **Two implementations.** You are right, and today's check does contain them: eleven stand-ins, including a rewrite of Node's path functions. They are scaffolding that counts what must go. In [the design](.mdmd/layer-3/engine-and-hosts.mdmd.md) the engine imports nothing from Node. Paths become one small module of its own, and anything a browser offers is used through the web-standard API that Node also has. Only three functions are written twice: list a folder, read a file, read a file's first bytes.
- **Git in a page.** I think it can be done. A repository's history is compressed files under `.git`, and a browser has both primitives built in (inflate and SHA-1). One reader of `.git` inside the engine would serve both surfaces, so the command line would stop needing the `git` program. Not known: whether the folder picker hands over `.git`, and the time and memory in a tab. I'm starting a probe with git as its oracle. TFS keeps no history on disk, so it has none on either surface.
- **WebForms and browser freedom.** That closes the folder-picker unknown. DNN and mojoPortal, the two .NET codebases we grade on, are WebForms applications, so the shape is already measured. I have not checked whether we draw a page's `fetch` to the server method that answers it.
- **SQLite in a browser.** It runs there, as WebAssembly. The page must hold the whole database in memory, which is where the 854 MB came from.
- **Stored form.** Recorded as the direction: binary as the stored form, text exported on demand, and the page saving a copy of itself as the portable thing. Browser storage is only what lets a scan survive a closed tab.

## Ignore patterns

I'm adopting your model, and it is simpler than what I built.

- **One list decides.** It has `.gitignore` syntax and behaviour. My five layers of precedence collapse into it, plus two fixed things never scanned: the version-control folder and our own output.
- **Found ignore files are offered:** Use, Add or Ignore. Two measured wrinkles for the import:
  - A folder can hold many ignore files at different depths, so each line is rewritten with its folder in front.
  - `.tfignore` differs in three small ways, so it is translated on import.
- **Defaults are optional prefills.** One is the Visual Studio list. I'd add a second, generated from what the folders hold (a `bin` beside a project file). The preview matters here: the Visual Studio list leaves out 60 real source files of DNN.
- **Nothing is applied silently.** This reverses my morning decision that the build-output rules apply by default.
- **The preview stays.** What each line leaves out, with counts, updates as the list changes.

## Starting now

1. The pre-scan page rebuilt on your ignore model.
2. One engine batch, so the history is rebuilt once:
   - test rules per language
   - the comment guard
   - the PowerShell path
   - a SQL spelling
3. The git-in-a-page probe.
4. The pull-back's motion measured on a complex scene.
``````

``````markdown
## Git in a page: yes

- **One reader of `.git`, about 770 lines of plain JavaScript, runs unchanged under Node and in a browser tab.** On 10,348 commits across this repository and eslint it gives git's own answers with no difference. I reran its comparison here myself: 1,550 commits, 46,261 changed paths, zero differences.
- **A page does receive `.git`** through a folder input, measured under automation. To confirm by hand, open [probe.html](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/git-in-a-page/probe.html) on a machine of your own and pick any git repository; it answers in one line.

- **The existing library is not the answer.** isomorphic-git was about 16 times slower and peaked at 2.8 GB on this repository.
- **One gap matters:** renames that git detects by similarity, which are 14% of this repository's renames. I've sent the builder to port git's own estimate and hold it to git on every commit, so the history says exactly what it says today.
- **A consequence for the design:** today each historical commit is written to a scratch folder, about 15,000 to 22,000 git processes per rebuild by the builder's estimate. With this reader a commit's tree can be read like any other folder, so history becomes "the same scan, run over many folders" on both surfaces.
- **TFS confirmed from Microsoft's documentation:** no history is readable offline in either kind of workspace.
``````

``````markdown
## Still rough

Being fixed now:
- Block titles wrap over three lines.
- A note above the list is too long.
- The code sign needs its count beside it.
- One of the builder's pictures contains its own test lines.

## One fork for you: where the command line keeps its list

The page keeps its list in itself. The command line needs a place. My recommendation combines three things, so that a scan still leaves no trace unless you choose one:

1. **The list travels with the scan's result.** The saved page, or the command line's output, holds the list it was made with, so a rescan starts from it.
2. **`--list <file>`** names a list kept anywhere you like.
3. **A list file you place in the folder yourself is offered like any found ignore file** (Use, Add or Ignore), never applied silently.

The alternatives are to keep it only in your own settings folder (never travels with the code), or to name it in `.live-docs.config.json` (travels and is reviewed like code, but is a file in the repository, and a TFS folder without that config has none).

When the page settles I'll send blind testers over it, reported by actions and routes first.
``````

``````markdown
The readers' batch is back and accepted after I read its key changes. It is held unmerged with the other engine changes, and an independent reviewer is now trying to break it.

## What it did

- **Comment guard, answering your "how did that lapse".** A standing test of 187 cases covers every reader: a construct inside a comment must yield nothing, while the same construct written live beside it still does. 44 cases were failing. They are fixed with one comment-blanking step per syntax, not one patch per pattern.
- **What that removed on real code.** The builder read every lost line and each was inside a comment:
  - mojoPortal: 58 CSS references, 67 element ids and 4 config keys.
  - DNN: 86 C# connections.
- **PowerShell path.** A relative path without its extension now resolves to the `.ps1` first, as your Windows run showed. Your console output is kept as the Windows record. Nothing moved on the four PowerShell codebases.
- **SQL spelling.** `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, backticked names, temporary tables, virtual tables and triggers are read correctly, graded by SQLite itself. mojoPortal's SQLite schema went from 0 of 40 tables declared to 40 of 40. The T-SQL and PL/SQL graders did not move.

## Two rulings of mine

- **16 true C# connections on DNN are no longer drawn.** They existed only because a doc comment named the type. A comment's reference is not a use by the code, so they stay gone until the reader can follow an inherited member, which is how the compiler finds them.
- **I kept the SQL fix although it exposes an older fault.** The SQL reader binds names as if the whole workspace were one database. mojoPortal keeps one data layer per database, so declaring its SQLite tables gained 94 true links and 243 links to another database's script. Before the fix that schema declared no tables at all, which was simply wrong. "Which database does a script belong to" is now measured and is the next reader piece.

## What happens next

1. The reviewer reports on the batch (about an hour).
2. The test rules' second part builds on it: test projects in .NET, test-only modules in Rust, tSQLt test classes.
3. I merge all engine changes together, run the full browser suite, rebuild the history once (about 100 minutes), then run both gates.

That will run past midnight UTC. I'll propose closing the day's record then, and `main` moves only if everything passes.

Also out: the blind testers on the pre-scan page and the skip-unseen-cards measurement.
``````

``````markdown
- **The floor I asked for:** with unseen cards skipped and our two forced restyles removed, the stall is still 0.3–0.4 s, or 1.1–1.6 s on the weak machine. Those forced restyles turned out to cost nothing extra; they only moved the same work earlier.
- **My idea is dropped.** Each card holding its own look would buy the same half for far more work.
- **The real cost:** about 50 microseconds per element, across roughly 7,500 elements, each time the look changes. The builder's reading is that every element under the map is recomputed because it inherits sizes from the map, whether or not it uses them.

The next piece measures that directly: how many of those elements actually change, and how many are recomputed only because of what they inherit. If it holds, the sizes will be delivered only to the elements that draw text. That would be a refactor that must leave every still identical to the pixel, and it would help your original arrival and zooming by hand alike.
``````

``````markdown
- **Add and Ignore were never pressed** in any of the 33 runs.
- **A caution from the test's author:** the task said "also go by the ignore files", which may have nudged them towards Use.

Your three choices stay. I've sent the page back to change how they are worded and how they can be undone:

- **Empty list:** replacing and adding are the same act, so there is one button to take the files in, plus Ignore.
- **List with lines:** each button says what it does to them, in your own verbs: replace (with how many lines would go), add, leave alone.
- **Undo:** every change the page makes to the list can be undone, by Ctrl+Z and by an undo tool. Designers call this forgiveness: an action with a way back needs no warning.
- **Notice:** it keeps the other choices reachable after one is taken.

## Other stumbles, also being fixed

- **A line that leaves out the team's own code went unnoticed.** A row shows only its three largest file kinds, so the Visual Studio line `**/[Pp]ackages/*` showed `.dll · .xml · .pdb` and hid 11 `.cs` files of DNN's own installer code. Code kinds will come first in every row, and the top line will carry a count of code left out.
- **The totals scrolled out of view** when a tester went looking for line 38 of 51. They will stay in view.
- **An offer vanished under the pointer** after the other was taken, and the next click landed in the list.

## What went well

- The cold-start question was answered from the first picture in one action by all four testers.
- "Use the folder's own ignore files" took three actions each.
- Finding which Visual Studio lines leave out real code took 8 to 14 actions and found `[Ll]og/` and `[Dd]ebug/`.
- Every total the page showed agreed with git's own count.

## What these runs cannot show

The testers read every picture in full, which a person will not. There was no real browser reload or download, and the folder had 17,244 files, not your 100,000. All 33 runs cost $0.86.
``````

``````markdown
Two things came back in the last few minutes: an independent review of the readers' batch, and the fixed pre-scan page.

## The independent review broke the readers' batch twelve ways

The reviewer brought back 64 failing tests and changed no product code. It also reproduced the batch's own numbers and found one stale figure in an earlier record.

**Fixed before the landing** (the batch's builder is on these now):

- **A regression the batch introduced.** `./build.v2`, beside both `build.v2` and `build.v2.ps1`, now resolved to the `.ps1`. PowerShell tries `.ps1` first only for a name with no extension of its own.
- **C# `#if false` sections are read as code.** There are six in DNN, one of them a whole file. The builder had left this out as "not a comment". I ruled with the reviewer, for the literal `false` only: the C# specification says such code is never read.
- **Attributes inside a `<script>`'s text are read as markup.** mojoPortal gets two false element ids from a JavaScript comment.
- **Smaller points:**
  - positions shift after an emoji in a comment;
  - two details of the HTML and CSS specifications;
  - several rules had no test that fails without them.

**Not fixed tonight, listed openly.** Everything that needs the SQL reader to know which dialect a script is in. The largest example is PostgreSQL's schema `public`, which is taken for a T-SQL reserved word. On mojoPortal's PostgreSQL install scripts that would be 0 tables declared against 517, if those scripts were read. Those scripts are `.config` files today, so the loss in what is read now is 3 declarations. These cases go into one ledger of known differences, which fails if a listed case stops being wrong or a new one appears.

**The larger point.** This is the second review today to break "no reader reads what a comment or a string holds", by different means each time. Our guide's rule for that is to stop fixing case by case and measure a design that does not need the guarantee. So the next reader piece is a design: readers that work from a real parse of their language, and a dialect for each SQL script. That is the same question as which database a script belongs to.

## The pre-scan page is fixed and back with the testers

- **Your three choices, relabelled.** With lines already in the list the notice now reads "Replace your list (removes 47 lines)", "Add them to your list", "Leave your list alone" ([picture](.claude/worktrees/agent-acb90fe6d791704f2/AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/leave-out/choice/screenshots/nested-ninth-3-choices-on-a-list.png)). At 21:18:44 UTC:
``````

``````markdown
Answers in your order, then where tonight's work stands.

## Doc comments

Nothing to take back: the lapse was real, and you named the complication correctly. The rule is narrower than "strip all comments".

- **A doc comment is still read as what it is.** A C# `///` summary, like its counterparts in the other languages, still becomes the description on a card.
- **Code-looking text inside a comment is no longer read as code.** A `<ProjectReference>` or `<script src>` inside a comment draws nothing.
- **One consequence touches doc comments.** `<see cref="X"/>` used to draw a dependency on `X` and no longer does. On DNN that removed 16 connections the compiler agrees are real; it binds them through an inherited member, which our C# reader does not follow yet. I ruled that a documentation reference is not a use by the code. If you want them shown, I would draw them as a citation, as a Markdown document citing a file already is.

## Scanning database projects

Yes: one database, one scanned object.

- **Why.** In SQL the database is the space in which a bare name has one meaning; `Orders` in two databases is two tables. Our reader treats everything in one scan as one database, so a scan is most accurate when it holds exactly one.
- **Today's limit.** Lines are drawn only inside one scan. A C# file that names a procedure or table is connected to the script that declares it only when both are scanned together.
- **Guidance for now:**
  - A folder with one database's scripts: scan it alone, or together with the one application that owns it.
  - A folder with several databases: scan each database's folder separately. Scanned together, same-named tables get tied to each other, which is mojoPortal's 243 false links.
- **Where it should go** (none of it built):
  - Each database is a piece on the World Map, as you said this morning: "each world map object is a recursively scanned directory". Today the World Map is still a board written by hand in a file.
  - A line between two pieces is drawn only where the code names the other piece, for example `Billing.dbo.Invoices`, a linked server, or a procedure called from C#. That keeps to your rule against "a hailstorm of false positive links".
  - A reader finds the database boundaries inside one scan (one database project file is one database), so nobody splits a solution by hand. This is the next reader piece, measured on public codebases first.

## "Update all" on the World Map

I think it is feasible on both surfaces, and it is recorded as an idea in your words.

- **In a page.** Chromium lets a page keep its handle to a folder you picked and ask for access again later; pressing the button is the consent the browser wants. Each piece would show whether its folder is still reachable. A copy of the page carried to another machine would ask to be pointed at the folder again.
- **On the command line.** The stored scan keeps its folder's path and re-scans whatever still exists.
- **Not known.** Whether a page opened from disk keeps those handles after the browser closes. Today's research showed such a page keeps its scan data, but handles were not tried, and automation cannot press a folder picker, so that check needs a person.

## Exploring while the scan runs

I timed one scan of this repository just now (1,292 files, command line, busy machine, 21 seconds):

| Stage | Time | What exists at its end |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Listing the folder | 3 s | file names |
| First pass over every file | 5 s | every file's names collected; no file finished |
| Files finished one at a time | 12 s | each file with its symbols and what it uses, in path order |

So it is not one scan-wide batch. After roughly the first two fifths, files could reach the map one by one. This is one repository and one run; I have not split the time for a scan in a tab.

- **"What uses this file" is complete only at the end.** Any later file may add to it.
- **Files arrive in path order,** so the map would grow one folder at a time. For git repositories we can now read the order of first commits inside a page, which is close to Obsidian's creation order. TFS folders have no such order.

What it would need:

1. **The scan on a thread of its own.** Today a scan in a tab freezes the page for its whole length. On a two-core VM the scan and the 3D motion would still share the processor.
2. **The Explorer drawing facts as they arrive.** Today it loads a finished graph. The 3D map already grows a pinned group from its seed, so adding to a running picture is not new to it.
3. **An honest sign that the picture is partial,** such as "412 of 1,292 files". Designers call this progressive rendering, and its known risk is things moving under the pointer. 3D absorbs arrivals; in 2D a late neighbour would shove the cards you are reading, so 2D needs its own rule.

Your rule about ignoring input while 3D populates was about a transition of a few seconds. A scan lasts a minute or more, so I would let people in.

**My recommendation is to see it before building it.** A trial with no engine change: feed a finished graph to the 3D map in the measured rhythm (a pause for the first pass, then file by file), as a Tuning switch, with path order and first-commit order to compare. It shows you whether it looks alive or "ugly and stupid-looking" before any engine work. I plan it as the first Explorer piece of the next dev day. Tonight the browser and the processor are spoken for by the engine's landing.

## Where tonight stands

- **The arrival's stall is understood, not cured.** It is not waste: 5,909 of the 6,474 elements restyled really change. A third round refuted my inheritance idea and showed that hiding the folders' cells and the descriptions saves nothing measurable. What would cut the work to about a third is building each card's looks as separate parts that are swapped. The next step is a prototype on one card.
- **One small fix is merged.** Look Before's zoom-out now waits for the look change's fade to finish. On the slowed machine the motion on `pathfind.ts` draws 14 frames where it drew 3 or 4.
- **Your preview (port 8999) was rebuilt at 21:13 UTC** with that fix. I am still running the camera, Tuning and legend browser tests on it.
- **The engine's landing** waits on the fixes from the independent review. An early browser check of the gathered engine passed 61 of 61. The full suite, one history rebuild and both gates follow, and will run past midnight UTC.

The full measurements of the look change are in [section 6 of the pull-back record](AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/arrival-pull-back/README.md).
``````
