``````markdown
Taken as direction: the page is designed for a person, the command line for an agent, and both carry everything.

It fits what is already written. The guide says whatever a person can do in the page is also available through commands or exported data. Your October 3 words, "exports for people, not doors for agents", are the P.S. you just repeated, and the Markdown bundle stays. What is new is whose needs shape the command line. That changes four things in the work in hand:

- **The command line never asks a question mid-run.** Before a scan it reports the ignore files it found and what each would leave out, as data. The decision goes in by a flag or a list file, and nothing is applied silently. This is the recommendation I put to you at 19:32 UTC on where the command line keeps its list, and your framing is the reason for it.
- **Its output is written for an agent to read.** That means stable structure, bounded size, a count of what was cut, and refusals in one line that say what to do instead.
- **Every new page feature gets its command-line twin in the same piece of work.** "Update all" and facts arriving during a scan would each have one.
- **Blind agent runs become a fair test there.** On the page, a model stands in for a person, with the bias you described. On the command line the model is the real user, so its action counts and wrong answers mean what they say. I will check what the record already holds on such runs before proposing any.

One caution from your own words on October 3: an agent with the repository open had "no need for it whatsoever". So the command line has to earn its place with answers that reading files does not give cheaply: what depends on this, what is reachable, what is dead, how it changed.

Also since my last message: the camera, Tuning and legend browser tests pass on the merged day branch, 31 of 31, so the Look Before fix in your preview is verified.
``````

### Root's reply to Turn 8, as delivered

A short line, then the reply.

At 21:26:49 UTC:

``````text
Reading your three points against the record first (what the comment guard removed exactly, and what the World Map design already says about suggestions), then I'll answer.
``````

At 21:27:31 UTC:

``````markdown
## The author's word

Agreed, and I reverse my ruling: a doc comment's reference to another type will be drawn.

- **One correction to how you read me.** For those 16 pairs the compiler agrees the two files are connected through code. Separately, the compiler also resolves every `cref` itself and warns when one names nothing. So there is an independent answer key for exactly this, which makes it safe to read on purpose.
- **How I'd draw it: as its own kind of line, "the author says these are related".** It shows on the map and in the lists. But "what is reachable from here" and "is this dead code" keep following only what the code does. Example: a class named only in `/// <see cref="OldParser"/>` should still be reported as unused. Say so if you'd rather it count as a plain dependency.
- **Breadth.** Other languages have the same construct, such as `{@link}` in JSDoc and Javadoc or Rust's doc links. Each gets read the same way, checked against its own tool.
- **Not in tonight's landing.** It needs the compiler's `cref` answers committed as a test first, and a place in the stored facts for "a code file cites a type". It is the first reader piece of the next dev day. Until then those references are not drawn on `main`.
- **Not known yet:** how many of the other 70 DNN pairs the guard removed were explicit references and how many were a type's name in ordinary comment text. That count comes with the piece.

## Suggestions when wiring the World Map

Yes, and it fits a rule you set on October 9: a suggestion never creates a thing. Your description gives that rule its form.

- **Completion from what each scan found.** When you wire two pieces, the field offers each piece's own procedures, tables, endpoints and symbols. Designers call this recognition over recall: you pick from what exists instead of typing a name from memory.
- **Suggestions ranked by evidence, never drawn until accepted.** Your "ghosts" are one half already: a name a file uses that its own scan cannot find. A ghost in one piece and a declaration of the same name in another is a suggested wire, for example a procedure named in a C# string and a database piece that declares it. Each suggestion shows the line of code it rests on.
- **None of this is built.** I've written it onto the World Map work item in your words.

## Parity, configuration, and pipelines

This does raise the right question. My answer so far, as a proposal:

- **Parity through shared files.** Every choice a person makes in the page becomes a file the command line reads, and the page can write and read that same file. That covers the leave-out list, the board with its pieces and accepted wires, and the settings. The page is the comfortable editor; an agent or a pipeline edits the same files as text.
- **Equivalent, not identical.** A person looks at the map. An agent asks a question and gets a bounded answer. A pipeline does neither: it produces and checks.
- **What a pipeline adds to the command line's requirements:**
  - Exit codes that are a contract.
  - The same bytes for the same input, so results cache and diff. This answers a question the format research left open, namely what that property is for.
  - No prompts, no network.
  - It must run on Windows build agents.
- **A pipeline is also "update all" without a person.** It scans on every change and publishes the filled page as an artifact that people open. We already do this for this repository: `pages.yml` scans it and publishes its map.
- **Making parity checkable.** Today the guide states the rule and nothing enforces it. I'd keep a table of what a person can do in the page against the file or command that does the same, with gaps listed by cause. A new page feature without its twin would then show up in review. That is a piece for the next dev day, and the record search now running feeds it.

I'm recording this turn now. The readers' fixes and the blind testers' second round are still out.
``````

``````markdown
The readers' fixes are back from the independent review, read and merged into the gathered engine branch. The check without the browser is running there now; the history rebuild starts when it passes.

## What the fixes did

Of the reviewer's 64 failing cases, 33 are fixed and 31 are held in a ledger of known differences, each with its cause.

- **PowerShell:** `./build.v2` no longer resolves to `build.v2.ps1`. The `.ps1` is tried first only for a name with no extension, as PowerShell itself does.
- **C#:** code under a literal `#if false` is no longer read. On DNN that removes 11 connections, none of which the compiler recognises.
- **Markup:** the text inside `<script>`, `<style>`, `<textarea>` and `<title>` is no longer read as page elements. This costs a few ids that only exist inside script text: 12 on nopCommerce, 8 on mojoPortal, 5 on DNN.
- **Positions:** a file containing an emoji or other rare character no longer shifts every position after it.
- **The ledger fails in three ways:** when a listed case starts working, when a new wrong case appears, or when a listed case stops being run.

The 31 ledgered cases wait on two designs, not more patches: the SQL reader knowing each script's dialect (PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQLite), and a markup reader that parses a page's embedded code with that code's own parser. Razor and Web Forms code blocks are in the second group.

All graders are unchanged except the C# figure above. The builder's full check passed: 3,602 unit and 500 integration tests, and 50 of 50 page-versus-command-line cases.

## A change to the landing order

I'm dropping the full browser suite that was planned before the history rebuild. The early check at 21:06 UTC already ran the tests that a changed map can break, and the history tests cannot pass until the rebuild exists anyway. The order is now:

1. The check without the browser (running).
2. One history rebuild, about 100 minutes, with its comparison read cause by cause.
3. Both full gates on the final state.

`main` still moves only if both gates pass, about fifty minutes sooner than the old order. It will be after midnight UTC.

One follow-up I am not holding the landing for: the ledger sits among the engine's source files, so editing it alone would force a history rebuild. It moves next to the tests with the next reader piece.
``````

### Root's reply to Turn 10, as delivered

A short line, then the reply.

At 23:17:32 UTC:


At 23:18:20 UTC:

``````markdown
Yes on both counts, and the base64 part is already how the research probe did it. The probe's saved page carries the facts as base64 in a block the browser never runs. At 53,880 files that page is 10 MB and is built in under half a second.

Here are the journeys I took, each ending in a dead end to avoid and what it asks of us now.

## 1. A colleague sends you their saved page

An HTML file is a program. Opening someone's saved page means running their copy of our script, which anyone could have edited, with our no-network policy stripped out.

- **Dead end:** the saved page as the only thing people can share.
- **Instead:** the page stays the convenient portable form, as you leaned. But your own trusted copy must also be able to take a colleague's page and read only its data block, never running it.
- **This is also the security case for base64.** Its alphabet has no `<`, so the data can never end its own block and become markup. Denser encodings save up to a quarter of the size and lose that property.

## 2. Someone hostile hands a file to a reader

"Ironclad" has to become properties a test can fail:

- **The reader always finishes.** For any bytes at all, it returns facts or refuses, in time and memory bounded by the file's size. The classic attack is a decompression bomb: a few kilobytes that claim to unpack into gigabytes. The standing test is a fuzzer that mutates valid files.
- **The file is pure data.** No expressions, filters, plugins or references to anything outside itself. HDF5's filter plugins and PDF's scripts are where those formats' vulnerabilities live.
- **One set of facts has exactly one byte form, and anything else is refused.** Otherwise two readers can interpret one file differently, and an attacker shows each a different map. The probe already writes the same bytes for the same facts, so this costs little.
- **Every string in the file is untrusted text.** Three consumers can be hurt:
  - A viewer that renders a name as HTML.
  - An exporter that writes a file named `../../something`. Our own Markdown export writes files named by scanned paths.
  - An agent that reads a doc comment saying "ignore your instructions". With agents as the command line's first user, that last one is ours to handle: every text should carry where it came from.

## 3. A second project writes its own reader

- **Dead end:** the format is whatever our code does.
- **Instead:** a written specification, test files with their expected facts, and a small second reader written from the specification alone by someone who never saw our code. That is our oracle rule applied to ourselves.
- **Text and binary must convert both ways without loss.** The text form then defines what the binary means, serves agents, and is the archive if every binary reader is lost. This is the main answer to "new file formats take on some risks".
- **Other tools get bridges, not adoption.** The model (files, symbols, typed connections, scans) is what others would want; the byte layout is one encoding of it. I'd export to formats other tools already read.

## 4. Two people plan on the same World Map

Scan facts can be regenerated. Authored plans cannot, and people need to review, diff and merge them.

- **Dead end:** authored planning stored inside the opaque binary.
- **Instead:** authored content stays text, joined to scans by stable names. Your "ghosts" already cover the hard case: a plan pointing at something a rescan no longer finds becomes a ghost and is not silently dropped.

## 5. The map leaves the building

A shared file discloses whatever the scan captured: paths, names, descriptions, and some values. The estate sample's map already shows a staging address read from a config file.

- **Dead end:** a format that can hold anything and says nothing about what it holds.
- **Instead:** each scan states what kinds of facts it carries and what was left out, never stores file contents or absolute paths, and can be shared in part.

## 6. Five years on

Some choices become permanent at first release. Engineers call these one-way doors: the identifying bytes at the start of the file, the extension, the ordering rules, the hash.

- **The format's name should not contain the product's name,** since you may rename the product.
- **Don't split it off early.** Standardising before a second real consumer exists freezes mistakes.
- **What costs nothing now:** keep the reader and writer as one module that imports nothing else of ours, with no third-party code inside. Splitting it off later is then a move, not a rewrite.

## What I'd hold us to before any product code

1. One module, importing nothing.
2. Lossless both ways with text, as a test.
3. The always-finishes property, fuzzed.
4. Authored content kept as text.
5. A statement of contents in every scan.

## What I don't know

- **Whether an existing model for code facts fits ours** well enough to borrow its shape. Sourcegraph's SCIP is the one I'd read first.
- **What our readers capture from config values.** That needs an audit.
- **The layout is still a research probe,** measured on one real codebase and a five-times repeat of it.

I'll put these on the stored-form work item as requirements, not decisions.

On tonight's landing: the history rebuild has not reported yet.
``````

### Root's reply to Turn 11, as delivered

At 23:37:45 UTC:

``````markdown
Answering your three points now; the landing run has passed its check without the browser and is in the browser suite.

## Detached pieces

No, it doesn't preclude that. Two different things are involved:

- **Detached** means the folder is not reachable from where the page runs. The scan's facts are all still in the data, so the piece opens fully: its Local Map, symbols, connections and history.
- **A ghost** arises only when a newer scan of a piece no longer contains something a plan points at. A plan pointing into a detached piece is not a ghost, because the target is still in the stored facts.

A team static site is the case where every piece is detached for every viewer, and it is already how this repository's own site works: a pipeline scans and publishes, and the published page never touches a folder. The World Map design already describes this as the pipeline "builds detached and publishes".

- **What detachment costs:** only updating. As far as I know nothing else reads the folder at viewing time today. If we later show the line of source a connection rests on, that would need the folder or a stored copy of the line.
- **What it owes the viewer:** an honest mark on each piece saying when it was scanned and from what, since it cannot refresh itself.
- **"Innards" means facts, not source.** The stored form holds names, descriptions and connections, never file contents, so a hosted site cannot show code it was never given.

## Guarantees as the waiting text

Agreed, and I'd make it stronger than rotating phrases: each line should be true of this very run and tied to the phase in progress, so it doubles as the progress indicator. In the spirit of:

- While listing: "Reading file names only. Nothing is opened until you choose."
- While scanning: "4,212 of 10,946 files read. Your code is read as text, never built or run."
- Throughout: "0 bytes sent anywhere. This page cannot use the network."
- While saving: "Writing one file, where you chose."

Three rules keep it from becoming security theatre:

- **Counted, not asserted.** "0 bytes sent" comes from the page's actual state.
- **Each line is backed by a test in our gate.** If we cannot test a claim, we don't show it.
- **We say "this page", never "your machine".** We don't control the browser or its extensions.

Designers call this operational transparency: showing the work as it happens builds more trust than a spinner. It is the one deliberate exception to "almost no text of our own on screen", so that rule in the design system changes in the same commit that builds it. On the command line, the same statements belong in the scan's stored result.

## Installed like an app

Yes, and it needs no wrapper. What you describe is a Progressive Web App: the same static page plus a small manifest file, which Edge and Chrome then offer to install. Architects call the result local-first software: your data lives in files you own, and the app works with no server.

That gives three forms of one page, each trading trust in a server for convenience:

| Form | Trust needed | What it adds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One HTML file opened from disk | none | works anywhere |
| The same page on a site (public, or your team's) | that site | shareable links, hosted maps |
| Installed from that site | that site, on every update | its own window, remembered folders, opening our data files by double-click |

- **What the installed form buys us.** Remembered folder access is what "update all" needs. Opening a data file by double-click is the "open a colleague's map in your own trusted copy" case from my last message.
- **Where the devil is.** An installed app is whatever its site serves, and by default it updates silently. Whoever controls the site controls code that holds your folder permissions. For "nothing leaves the machine", the install source should be your own intranet site or a locally served copy, with updates only when you accept them.
- **It cannot be installed from a file on disk,** so the single file stays the zero-trust form.
- **Electron or similar would mean** installers, code signing, per-system builds and, for Electron, shipping a browser of over 100 MB. I see no need.

What I don't know, because I am recalling browser behaviour I have not tested here:

- Whether remembered folders and file opening work as documented on your Edge under your company's policy.
- Whether Chromium still requires a background script for installing.
- Whether Chromium's signed, versioned app bundles are usable outside managed devices; they would remove the silent-update problem.

Settling these takes a small probe that a person must click through. I've listed it for the next dev day, not started.
``````

The audiences I hope to serve with this are wide, and I really do have wonderful colleagues who still use the heck out of browser bookmarks (which is great! It's fine!). It occurs to me that the "Install" for that honestly just obfuscates the fact that you're reaching out to a static site rather than a local file, which is precisely  the opposite of what I want: I was hoping that a local copy could be launched like an app. It's clear to me that I still have tradeoffs to reason about and grapple with. I was definitely mistaken in my mental model of what was going on with those PWAs.

``````text
>Electron or similar would mean installers, code signing, per-system builds and, for Electron, shipping a browser of over 100 MB. I see no need.

Nah nah none of that nonsense, agreed.
``````

### Root's reply to Turn 12, as delivered

At 23:59:02 UTC:

``````markdown
Your labels are better than mine, and you're right about the counter.

## The waiting text

- **Your wording is now the working wording:** "Listing Files... (count)", "Reading File Text... (scanned/listed)", "Saving... (% if available)". Plain progress and nothing else. "Reading File Text" already implies that nothing is run, without preaching it.
- **The "0 bytes sent" counter is withdrawn.** I did not mean watching traffic. The page is built so that it cannot use the network at all, and the zero would have been that fact stated as a number. But you read it as monitoring, and so would others. The sentence is yours: "everything happening now is happening on your machine."

On where else the guarantees belong: besides your one case, starting up on a file with a big scan inside, I can think of four. Each is a moment where a person decides whether to trust, which seems a better home than a moment of waiting:

- **Just before the browser asks for a folder.** One line on what the page will do with it. Designers call this permission priming.
- **The blank first screen.** It is the one place a newcomer reads before doing anything.
- **Just before saving or sharing.** One line on what the file holds: names and descriptions, not your code.
- **On opening someone else's data file.** It is read as data and nothing in it runs.

That is one line each, at most five places in the whole app.

## Launching a local copy like an app

Your corrected model is right in what matters. One detail: an installed web app starts from a cached copy and need not reach out on each launch. But it is still tied to a site, it checks that site for updates, and it cannot exist without one. It is a bookmark with a cache, which is the opposite of what you want. I'd drop it as our path and keep it only as an option for a team that already hosts its map on a site, where "install" is at least honest.

What "a local copy launched like an app" can be without a wrapper, as far as I know:

| Way to launch | What you get | What it lacks |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Double-click the HTML file, or a shortcut or bookmark to it | Opens in a browser tab; works for everyone | Looks like a web page |
| A shortcut that starts Edge or Chrome in app mode on the file | Its own window and taskbar icon, still just the local file | Someone must create the shortcut; the page cannot |
| Chromium's signed app bundles | A local, versioned install with no site | I believe this is limited to managed devices today |

Two things the local file cannot do by itself:

- **Open a data file by double-click.** That needs the operating system to register a file type. Dragging the file onto the page, or an Open button, covers it.
- **Remember a folder between launches.** This is the one that matters, because "update all" needs it, and I don't know whether a page opened from disk keeps that. A person has to test it.

One thing the local file does better: the copy you hold is the copy you run. It never changes unless you replace it.

All of this is recalled, not tested here. My recommendation: the single file stays the product, a shortcut is the "app", and the next step is a small probe you or I click through on Edge covering app mode on a local file and whether folders are remembered.

"Local-first software" is recorded as our term for it.

The browser suite is still running; its result is due around 00:25 UTC.
``````

``````markdown
The draft is written and on `main`: the full text is [the design philosophy](.mdmd/layer-1/design-philosophy.mdmd.md), and its short form is a new section of [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md#design-philosophy). The wording is on trial until you've corrected it. The three stale waits from this morning are stopped.

## The purpose and five values

**Purpose:** Live Documentation exists so that a person's understanding of their own software does not run away from them. That is your own sentence from October 8.

| Value | In short |
| --- | --- |
| **A person can see it** | Read at a glance and explored without getting lost, by someone who is not a software person; almost no words of our own |
| **The map does not lie** | Nothing drawn that the code does not make; what was unread, left out or hidden is said; a suggestion is never a fact |
| **It is yours** | Local-first: your machine, a copy you hold, code read as text and never run; your word honoured, nothing done in silence, everything reversible |
| **It meets you where you are** | A weak machine, an unannotated codebase, an unplanned language; a person, an agent, a pipeline: each gets everything in its own form |
| **As simple as possible, and no simpler** | One source of facts, one engine, one way of doing each thing |

Each value in the document carries your words, what it has already decided, and what it costs. A value that has never made us give something up is a slogan.

**The method** is the part I think is most yours: the values pull against each other, and the design is found between them. When two conflict, bend each in turn, measure, choose the form that gives up least, and say which value paid. The document lists six trades already made that way, for example the installed web app refused in favour of the single file.

## Where your two sources landed

- **Your prompts.** I did not send a miner. `owner.md` already holds them curated, so I read the lead of all 249 entries and the product sections in full, and checked every quotation I used.
- **WCAG's four principles** are not the pillars. They govern interfaces and say nothing about whether the map is true, whose machine it runs on, or how big the thing has grown. They became the check inside two of the values, applied to all three audiences. For a pipeline, "understandable" means exit codes that are a contract.

## Is the request silly? No, with one distinction

Design values and working instincts are two lists. But they share one root: the same five values, turned on the workspace, produce the rules already in the guide. "The map does not lie" becomes "say what was read". So the new section states the values once, and the rest of the guide stays as their application.

## Work items: I recommend a field, not directories

I tested your hypothesis on the nine issues. Seven have a clear first value. The two that don't are the December showing, which is a date, and the work items themselves, which is process.

My recommendation is one field on each issue naming the value it serves first, with the board grouped by it. Reasons against directories:

- Four of the seven issues serve two values in earnest, and a directory allows one parent.
- The two non-value issues would need a folder that is no value.
- About 160 links in 50 files point at the issues where they stand.

I changed no work item; that form is yours to settle. I agree that tasks stay inside their issue's file.

## What it still owes

A reader who has not seen my five should go through your own turns and look for a value that fits none of them. I'd run that first on the next dev day.

Your note on cleaning the git history is recorded as an idea, very low priority, to be started only when you ask for it by name.

## Closing the day

The day has turned and the evening's work is landed, so I propose closing this record: the summary, the narrative chapter, Memory brought level, and this day's finished worktrees and scratch removed. Say the word, or tell me what you'd rather see first.
``````
